algorithms that are capable of classifying current user activity. The prototype version of

74
this module used accelerometer data to perform the detection of very simple activities,
such as walking or running.
SocialSense was also thought to be able to support the use of several medical devices.
Our prototype implementation supported a small heart beat monitor (also depicted
in Figure 4.1), whose communication was based on the Bluetooth protocol. The use
of these “external” devices required an intermediate device (mobile phone or wireless
node) that supported both the external protocol (Bluetooth) and communication with
the middleware.
4.1.2 SocialSense’s prototype implementation
The proposed implementation of the SocialSense model supported 6LowPAN as well
as traditional WSNs. Communication between proxies and middleware was based on
TCP/IP and RESTful web services, supporting XML and JSON as the data formats.
The communication between proxies and sensor nodes was implemented independently,
using any protocols that were appropriate in order to allow interoperability between
diﬀerent networks. Web services at the “bottom level” were used by the proxies for
posting new data to the middleware. The web services provided basic functionality
for storing data (which could be sensory or localization data) within the system in a
private database. Examples of such “low level” web services called by proxy devices
were the postTemperature()and postLocalizationCoordinates()services, although there
were others of similar functionality. “Higher level” web services were used by web
interfaces and applications to retrieve ready-to-use, processed data from the middleware.
We also implemented a web interface (using HTML and Javascript) that allowed users
to see their own location and share data on their Facebook proﬁle. This web interface
used several of our framework’s web services, such as getTemperature(), getLocalization-
Coordinates() and getActivity() for retrieving the most recent data from our database
regarding a user’s current location, vital signs or activity. The middleware supported
a localization services based on the RSSIs of the received wireless packets and a trilat-
eration algorithm. In addition to the web services provided by our own middleware,
our web interface also integrated with the Google Maps API in order to display the
users’ location and activity by overlaying a map of our testbed and a small avatar. This
allowed it to update the user’s avatar position within the map, as shown in Figure 4.2.
The web interface also allowed users to select whose friends could have access to their own
activity and location and implemented a login-based security mechanism to access data
in our middleware. This login-based mechanism relied on Facebook’s own “access token”

75
Figure 4.2: SocialSense’s Web Interface, displaying an avatar’s location (circled red).
mechanism: when a user accessed our application, a web page asked for permissions to
fetch various types of data (the user’s friends, Facebook ID, etc.). In case the user
accepts these terms, an “access token” was generated by Facebook and sent to our
middleware by the user’s browser through HTTPS. This access token was then used
by our middleware to communicate with Facebook and fetch the user’s Facebook ID,
which is then compared to the existing IDs in our database. In case the ID exists on
the database, the user is given access to sensory data; otherwise, our webservice returns
nothing. Thus, only users registered within our system could fetch relevant data.
We developed a simple activity detection based on the data collected from the accelerom-
eter sensor of Zolertia Z1 motes [140]. With the accelerometer’s movement speed data,
we performed a simple signal frequency analysis tasks to classify the user’s activity as
“moving” or “not moving”. Our approach was similar to the one described in [141];
frequencies above the ones resulting from human walking motion were ﬁltered out and
a simple sum of the Fast Fourier Transformation (FFT) coeﬃcients was performed in
order to get a rough idea of the intensity of the movements being experienced. This
intensity was used to determine if the user was either walking or standing still. At the
same time, localization could be used to further distinguish the type of movement; for
example, if the user’s movement’s speed was less than 6 km/h then the system deter-
mined that the user was “walking”. Otherwise, the user was running on foot or driving
car, depending on the scenario. This classiﬁcation scheme, while very simple, was the
result of our early eﬀorts in establishing a working prototype.
In order to integrate activity detection with social networking, we developed a mashup

76
application that combined the services provided by our platform (through the middle-
ware web services) with the Facebook platform. The mashup periodically collected user
activity and sensor data and published it into the user’s personal wall. The results of
this integration are shown in Figure 4.3.
Figure 4.3: SocialSense’s activity and sensory information displayed on Facebook.
During SocialSense’s development, we found that the Zoleria Z1 motes were bulky and
not wearable. In fact, we discussed in the article that future iterations of our work
would beneﬁt from the use of more comfortable and smaller sensors. Our point of view
was that people-centric sensing should be built upon smartphone hardware, due to it
becoming increasingly more common and disseminated. However, at the same time,
applications could also beneﬁt from cheap and ﬂexible sensors, as long as they were
comfortable and lightweight and provided as many measurements as possible.
This was a paradoxical view at the time, since many interesting vital signs could still
only be measured with cumbersome technology. To better explain our vision, we dis-
cussed possible people centric sensing systems that provided “multiple” sensory con-
ﬁgurations. “Basic” solutions has only the most important hardware (a bracelet with
heart beat and accelerometer, for example) that acted as a direct bridge to the user’s
smartphone. However, users could acquire more sensors that provided greater capabil-
ity. A “sports” solution, for example, could provide more sensors for a better assessment
of physical exercise (e.g. oxygen saturation sensors). Such measurements would have
to be acquired by circumventing the need for cumbersome sensors, which provided an
interesting challenge from a System-on-a-Chip point-of-view. We also proposed some
ideas about combining the use of wireless technologies such as Bluetooth and NFC to
provide comfortable solutions for such sensing devices, as shown in Figure 4.4.

77
Figure 4.4: Hand-worn sensors that could be used in people-centric sensing.
4.1.3 In summary...
Although the obtained results presented in this early work were very simple, they pro-
vided us with an idea on how to seamlessly acquire and apply human-centric data within
a CPS. In fact, our team managed to detect a user’s activity based on the data from
wireless sensors and share this activity on a social network. Despite still being labeled
as a people-centric sensing approach, this work represented our ﬁrst steps into research
on HiTLCPSs. SocialSense can also be considered as a open-loop HiTLCPS.
The sensors used on SocialSense’s application prototype were still general-purpose wire-
less sensors. However, it was found that these were not adequate for real-world de-
ployments. It was around this time that we began to consider focusing on smartphone
technology, since it provided us with a device that accompanied the user throughout the
day and had, by itself, numerous sensors that could be used to acquire human-context
information.
Publications based on this section’s work
• D. Nunes, T.-D. Tran, D. Raposo, A. Pinto, A. Gomes, and J. S. Silva, “A web
service-based framework model for people-centric sensing applications applied to
social networking,” Sensors, vol. 12, no. 2, pp. 1688–1701, 2012

78
4.2 Achieving Human-Aware Seamless Handoﬀ
In a new work, titled Achieving Human-Aware Seamless Handoﬀ , we continued to ex-
plore the concept of people-centric sensing with a greater focus on smartphones and
Quality of Service (QoS). As we discussed back in page 71, some of the previously iden-
tiﬁed challenges in the state-of-the-art were privacy, managing intermittent connectivity
and the detection of context. As previously mentioned, we consider connectivity as a
client device’s ability to connect to the Internet in a manner that is adequate to support
its normal operation [139]. We also use the term bandwidth to refer to the capacity of
a network link [143] and the term latency to reference the round-trip delay time of a
connection [144].
This work attempted to contribute towards each of these challenges. In fact, it argued
that people-centric sensing applications present dynamic levels of QoS: some applica-
tions have sudden needs for bandwidth and latency to transmit large amounts of sensory
data; others may only require the exchange of short messages, while prioritizing connec-
tivity. Harvesting the ﬂexibility of diﬀerent connection interfaces in smartphones could
allow the device to better adapt to the circumstances; for example, WiFi might be used
to oﬄoad large quantities of data, cellular connections might beneﬁt connectivity and
even Bluetooth might be used to pass information in an opportunistic manner. On the
other hand, the device’s own status (e.g. battery level, available memory) should also
inﬂuence management of connections. While both these aspects had been previously
considered in research, there was another important factor that has yet to be consid-
ered. We deﬁned the concept of human user’s context or awareness as every aspect
of reality that directly related to the human condition, such as human emotions, activ-
ity, logical location or even social networking contacts. These were factors that could
be heavily considered in a network management’s control-loop. For example, a people-
centric sensing app could dynamically provide additional communication redundancy
when the user entered areas with known interference issues, or use multiple interfaces
simultaneously whenever the user was feeling anxious, in order to avoid further frustra-
tions resultant from intermittent connectivity. In fact, the stability of connections was
important, since if connectivity was intermittent the real-time component of the system
would be compromised [10].
4.2.1 Management of Connection Interfaces
We considered networking needs to be dynamic in people-centric sensing, since several
diﬀerent types of data could be sent depending on the context. When processing and
sending accelerometer data, for example, bandwidth and latency may be important

79
since we are dealing with large amounts of information. However, for receiving notiﬁ-
cations or sending location, connectivity is more important, so that the user does not
miss eventual events of interest. Thus, an intelligent network-selection mechanism could
manage networking performance, energy consumption and reduce intermittent connec-
tivity, resulting in more reliable systems. On the other hand, the availability of wireless
connections and their signal strength varies greatly as the user moves. In fact, the us-
age of multiple wireless mediums and network interfaces can not only contribute to a
better distribution of network traﬃc, to also to the increase of Internet coverage and
connectivity for mobile environments [145] and even to the energy eﬃciency of devices
[146]. While the overhead for acquiring sensing information could also be crucial in
many people-centric sensing apps, that analysis was outside of the scope of this work.
Several QoS and Quality-of-Experience (QoE)-aware interface selection mechanisms had
been previously proposed in the literature. Some solutions promoted savings in 3G
cellular traﬃc by trying to oﬄoad as much data as possible to WiFi. Through simple
history-based predictors that estimated future WiFi throughput, the transmission of
data was held until a WiFi network was available [145]. However, to do this, it was
necessary to know the application’s delay tolerance threshold and the QoS requirements.
Two types of solutions were envisioned for this purpose. Firstly, inference mechanisms
that use port information and binary names; e.g. packets corresponding to known email
ports or belonging to common email clients could be delayed [145]. Secondly, APIs used
by applications to transmit this QoS information [147].
Other works had expanded on this idea of oﬄoading data to WiFi. For example, in
[148] the utility and cost of network ﬂows were modeled to determine when to use the
3G interface. The authors also presented an implementation of this idea that took
advantage of both 3G and WiFi simultaneously by extending SCTP. On the other hand,
alternative approaches focused on speciﬁc goals set by the user. Such was the case in
MultiNets [149], a system that oﬀered diﬀerent switching policies to its users (energy
saving, data oﬄoading, or performance). Regarding this, the authors in [147] oﬀered a
diﬀerent perspective. They argued that network usage patterns may change quickly and
it is not feasible to expect users to understand their system’s behavior and constantly
update policies.
In [146], the energy cost of mobile connections was reduced through schedulers based on
energy models for diﬀerent radio interfaces and the continued communications history
of the user. While this was a step in the right direction for creating intelligent network-
selection mechanisms, the approach was limited in the sense that it was only concerned

80
with saving battery power. Additionally, it also only took into consideration the in-
terface’s energy consumption and the user’s usage history, disregarding application’s
requirements and user’s intentions.
4.2.2 A model for context-oriented network-selection mechanisms
As evidenced by the previous section, the state-of-the-art on networking interface man-
agement at the time this work was developed tended to focus exclusively on achieving
a speciﬁc objective or leaving the choice of selecting the best high-level goal to the
end-user, which was ineﬃcient and undesirable in terms of usability. We believed the
limiting factor was the lack of regard for the human context. Observing the develop-
ment of people-centric-sensing systems, it was natural to consider that it was not only
the device’s status (e.g. battery level) or the application’s QoS requirements that were
of importance to the problem at hand. The human himself should be a motivating
factor for considering which network interfaces should be used. Unlike MultiNets [149],
which merely considered user-deﬁned policies, we intended to go a step forward and
automate such policies in accordance to the human context. This idea could be applied
to a human’s psychological state (e.g. emotions), physiological condition (e.g. physical
activity) and real-world context (e.g. logical position), factors that can inﬂuence the
control-loop of the the interface-selection mechanism. Based on the previous discussion
there were three main aspects that needed to be considered:
• System-awareness - the status of the device, such as the available system’s
resources, its power status (charging / battery power / battery level) and link-
layer information (e.g. signal strength), among other variables;
• User-awareness - user’s should be able to impose restrictions (e.g. limiting
cellular traﬃc due to monetary costs), and deﬁne high-level goals (e.g. perfor-
mance, independently of battery-life), although these should not be required for
the system’s autonomy. Another aspect of user-awareness is the use of sensory
information to infer the user’s real-world context. As an example, emotional dis-
tress may prompt the smartphone to improve networking performance, in order
to promote a better mood;
• Application-awareness - as suggested in [145], [147] and [148], it is very im-
portant to consider the application’s requirements in terms of bandwidth and
connectivity.
While previous research had proposed architectures for implementations of interface
switching engines [149], these were bound to the limitations discussed above. With this

81
in mind, we proposed a general architecture for intelligent network selection engines,
of which we developed an experimental implementation. As far as we know, this was
the ﬁrst attempt at deﬁning a general model that combined in a logical and concise
manner the major ideas present in the literature, while also innovating by considering
the human-context.
Figure 4.5: The proposed network selection engine’s model
As we can see in Figure 4.5, our architecture considered three layers, the Application
Layer, the Network Selector Layer and the Kernel Layer. Applications existed at the
Application Layer and initiated connections with remote entities through the Ap-
plication Stream Classiﬁer , which evaluated the application’s requirements either
through direct methods, such as APIs, as proposed in [147] or indirect methods, such as
through mathematical models based on network throughout [150], or both. The Kernel
Layer included the system’s lower-level functions that provided information about the
system’s resources (e.g. battery-level, available memory), link-layer information (e.g.
wireless connection strength) and sensory data. This layer is usually accessed through
abstraction APIs provided by the device’s OS for higher-level layers. Handoﬀ mech-
anisms responsible for switching between the diﬀerent network interfaces exist at this
layer.
The network selection and evaluation process happened at the Network Selector
Layer, supported by the Application Stream Classiﬁer module and the User
Policy & Context Engine and System Status Engine modules. The User
Policy & Context Engine processed the policies deﬁned by the user (restrictions in
network interfaces, desire for high-performance or power savings) as well as processing
contextual information coming from the device’s sensors (e.g. emotional or activity
inference). The System Status Engine was responsible for acquiring and processing

82
relevant information from the Kernel Layer about the current condition of the system.
These three modules inﬂuenced the Network Selection Engine, which made decisions
about which networks and interfaces should the system connect to. These decisions were
translated to the Kernel Layer through the Handoﬀ Interfacer, which was responsible
for interacting with the handoﬀ mechanism in place. We believed this three-layer
design fully conceptualized the key problem in a way that previous designs had not
achieved. As evidenced by the state-of-the-art of the time, presented in section 4.2.1,
system-aware and application-aware designs had already been extensively considered.
Our design built on top of these concepts, while also introducing the user as a third
major factor.
Actual implementations of a User Policy & Context Engine would have to consider
techniques for modeling user context. At the time, we considered the concept of human
context to encompass every aspect of reality relating to the human condition, such as
human emotions, activity, location, social networking contacts, among others. The work
in [150] had previously expressed how much a user values the throughput of a network
ﬂow as U(x), a function of throughput x. Modeling U(x) as a logarithmic function for
elastic ﬂows (such as TCP downloads) had been common in networking literature, since
this function has a diminishing marginal rate of increase with increasing throughput
[148]. While our approach did not depend on the nature of this utility function, we did
consider a component that speciﬁcally translated the human context, H(k1,k 2,...kn),
where kn represented a factor that was relevant to the human (e.g. body temperature,
number of social networking contacts, etc.). Assuming a logarithmic behavior, we wrote
the utility function as:
U(x) =K1 log(x) +H(k1,k 2,...kn)
Considering now the cost for using each networking interface as a function C(x) and as-
suming thatxw andxg are the instantaneous throughputs of the WiFi and 3G interfaces
of the smartphone, respectively [148], an implementation of our model could maximize:
A(xw,xg) =U(xw +xg)−Cw(xw)−Cg(xg)
which can be rewritten as follows, to evidence the human factor:
A(xw,xg) =K1 log(x) +H(k1,k 2,...kn)−Cw(xw)−Cg(xg)

83
The design of utility functions for speciﬁc applications was outside of the scope of our
work. In particular, solutions for the human context function H(k1,k 2,...kn) had pre-
viously been extensively considered in the literature, often requiring advanced math-
ematical models or machine learning techniques. Nevertheless, we presented a simple
implementation of these concepts, discussed in section 4.2.4. We also noted that user
policies could also be represented by adaptive weights in the cost function C(x). For
example, we can assume Cg(x) to be a linear function:
Cg(x) =K2 +M1x
whereM1 is a weight representing the monetary cost established by the cellular provider
for using 3G traﬃc.
4.2.3 HappyHour - a case-study app
In this article, we performed an evaluation study that served as a proof-of-concept for our
human-aware model. To do so, we implemented a simple User Policy & Context Engine
based on the user’s movement and position. At the same time, we further validated the
usage of MultiPath Transmission Control Protocol (MPTCP) as a handoﬀ protocol, as
we will discuss in section 4.2.4.
This evaluation study was performed through an Android app that we implemented,
named HappyHour. It was based on the premise that people seek diﬀerent environ-
ments when socializing. Sometimes they may feel more cheerful and like partying in a
crowded place; other times, they just want to relax and pass the time while listening to
some music and talking with friends. Finding a place that ﬁts our mood is a traditionally
trial and error process that involves visiting several places in our surroundings, or a more
predictable approach of visiting regular places. Thus, people either spend a lot of time
trying to ﬁnd an adequate place that ﬁts their social group’s desires or resort to always
going to the same places. HappyHour enabled users to ﬁnd nightlife establishments in
a directed way, by allowing them to know what was happening around them in near
real-time. Through a map, as shown in Figure 4.6, users could check the establishments
of interest in their surroundings. The App aggregated the points of interest (POIs) in
clusters depending on the zoom level, for easy viewing.
By periodically aggregating the users’ GPS positioning and accelerometer readings, the
App could also show on the map an estimation of which areas were the most popu-
lated and those where people’s movements were more pronounced (e.g. due to dancing).
The movement and attendance were represented through heatmaps (as shown in Figure
4.6), colored layers surrounding the point of interest. The colors green, yellow and red

84
Figure 4.6: HappyHour Map screen
classiﬁed the amount of attendance or movement, with green representing low movemen-
t/attendance and red representing high movement/attendance. These heatmaps could
be toggled through two buttons located in the upper-right side of the screen: the left-side
button represented a crowd and activated the attendance heatmap, while the right-side
button represented movement. By clicking on a POI’s icon, the users could access its
description, currently scheduled events (e.g.,“80’s night”) and request navigation to its
location through an external navigation application.
This allowed users to pick areas with a more vibrant party vibe (more populated and
agitated) or calmer areas that are more prone to soothing environments (less populated
and agitated). In addition, users could also be informed about promotions and oﬀerings
(e.g. drinks 50% oﬀ) that were occurring in their surroundings through a notiﬁcations
system that allowed nightclub managers to send notiﬁcations to users within 700 meters
if their establishment. This system also had a management web interface (accessed
through Facebook) where nightclub / bar owners could create and edit events and
schedule notiﬁcations to be sent at a certain time. Thus, HappyHour oﬀered a business
model that gave establishments the opportunity to reach customers in a more direct
way. Figure 4.7 shows the application’s architecture. The Backend server was
responsible for management of users, establishments, events and notiﬁcations. The
server oﬀered its services in the form of RESTful web services, both to the Android
App and to the Management web interface. It also communicated with a database
where records of users, POI locations, descriptions and events were kept. The Android

85
Figure 4.7: HappyHour’s Architecture
App was responsible for the interaction with the end-user. It displayed a map where
the relevant points of interest were shown. It also was responsible for acquiring,
processing and sending GPS positions and accelerometer data. The Management
Web Interface (developed using HTML, Javascript, Dojo 1 and PHP) was restricted
to establishment owners, allowing them to manage events and notiﬁcations. A video
showing some of HappyHour’s functionality was available online at the time of this
document’s writing2.
Our User Policy & Context Engine supported a new HappyHour feature, named
FindMe, which intended to facilitate the process of meeting friends. Due to the constant
background chatter and movements of nearby people, it is often diﬃcult to communicate
meeting locations in crowded nightlife environments. Having previously registered his
friends on HappyHour, a user could enable the FindMe feature, triggering the backend
server to periodically check for the presence of friends in the surroundings. Whenever
this was the case, the users’ respective positions and activity were shared on each other’s
maps, in near real-time. For this experimental proof-of-concept, we implemented a sim-
ple activity classiﬁer that performed a FFT on the accelerometer’s data, measuring the
amount of movement. Depending on this amount, the user’s state was classiﬁed as ei-
ther resting, walking or dancing. Each of these activities was represented in the map
interface of the user’s friends as speciﬁc types of markers, placed at the user’s current
geographical position.
To avoid taxing the smartphone with this classiﬁcation process, we experimented with
data oﬄoading. FindMe automatically began sending the user’s GPS coordinates and
accelerometer data in near-real time to the remote backend server, where the classiﬁ-
cation process was performed. Empirically, we estimated that network disconnections
would often disrupt our application, particularly when users moved between diﬀerent
1https://dojotoolkit.org/
2https://youtu.be/sKyo8wRVPYA

86
WiFi networks. These disconnection periods could be as high as 10 seconds when losing
WiFi connectivity due to the reestablishment of TCP connections on the 3G interface.
To maintain an accurate evaluation on position and activity, the QoS requirements for
FindMe required these disconnection periods to be as low as possible. In order to min-
imize losses of data resultant from disconnections during the measuring process, we
devised a simple User Policy & Context Engine based on MPTCP that implemented
simple a H(kf,ka,ku) function.
The variable kf represented a boolean that translated whether or not the user was in
the vicinity of a friend. We deﬁned “vicinity” in terms of the user’s last known GPS
position, posu, which should be within 500 meters of the last known position of another
user in his list of friends, that is:
kf =
{
1 if posu≤ 500m
0 otherwise
On the other hand, ka was a boolean that represented whether or not the FindMe
function was currently active in the application’s settings. Finally, ku translated a
user-deﬁned policy to primarily use either WiFi, 3G or both interfaces simultaneously,
whenever sharing data in near-real time. Thus, the H(kf,ka,ku) function was based on
the following strategy:
if kf = 0 or ka = 0 then return “the user’s policy or context are not within func-
tionality parameters”
else
if ku =Both then
use Both interfaces whenever possible
else if ku =WiFi then
use WiFi as the primary interface
else
use 3G as the primary interface
end if
end if
Despite its simplicity, this implementation allowed us to consider several ideas previ-
ously discussed in section 4.2.2. The human’s context was represented by his geographic
positioning and activity.
Human desires were also taken into consideration through the enabling or disabling of
the FindMe functionality, as well as through the selected interface selection policy. This
policy had a considerable impact on the system’s usability: while using both interfaces

87
could achieve greater performance, it also resulted in additional battery consumption.
On the other hand, using primarily the 3G interface could provide greater connectivity,
but also induced additional monetary costs. Finally, using primarly WiFi oﬀered good
performance at a low cost, but this interface was prone to a limited connectivity range.
We will discuss these issues further in the next section.
4.2.4 Human-aware Handoﬀ
One important aspect of our architecture’s interface selection policy rested on the applied
handoﬀ mechanism. As we will discuss in section 5.2.3, “brute-force” handoﬀs, where
one network is simply disabled and another enabled, cause periods of interruption of
connectivity, which lead to losses of data and latency. To avoid this, we used MPTCP,
a modiﬁed version of TCP that naturally allowed for make-before-break handoﬀs. It
implemented multipath transport by pooling multiple TCP paths from disjoint network
interfaces within a single transport connection, transparently to the application [151].
Previous research had already used MPTCP to support mobility and reduce energy con-
sumption [152] [146]. These works showed that, due to energy constraints, it is often not
feasible to continuously use all available network interfaces and that mobile devices often
beneﬁt from having just a single primary connection active. Thus, interface selection
mechanisms are important to better manage energy consumption and performance.
Previous studies had also presented simulation experiments with other protocols,
namely comparing several mechanisms for improving MobileIP handoﬀ performance
[153]. These showed that standard MobileIPv6 presents a handoﬀ delay of approxi-
mately 814ms, with various of its improvement architectures achieving delays of around
270-450ms. In particular, S-MIP, a seamless handoﬀ architecture for MobileIP, achieved
a delay, as perceived by the sender, of a mere 100ms. However, other studies based on
real test-beds show that Round-Trip Time for standard MobileIP handoﬀ goes as high
as 8 seconds [154]. This sort of performance evaluation cannot be applied to techniques
such as the manipulation of routing tables proposed in [155], since these are not, in
essence, true handoﬀ operations.
While we implemented this experimental User Policy & Context Engine on a Nexus
5 Android phone with a MPTCP-enabled kernel, we decided to perform the handoﬀ
tests using UCLouvain’s Linux Kernel v0.88 implementation 3 and a laptop equipped
with a WiFi antenna and a 3G USB Dongle. The reason for this was simplicity, as the
Linux Kernel implementation allowed greater control over testing conditions. The laptop
3http://mptcp.info.ucl.ac.be/

88
hosted a virtual machine running an MPTCP-enabled Debian Squeeze and connected to
a remote host located on our faculty’s hosting farm running the same OS. The connection
was made through a private WiFi network supported by a Cable Internet backhaul,
oﬀering an upstream bandwidth of around 1 Mbps, and a public 3G network, oﬀering a
bandwidth between 0.2 and 0.4 Mbps. The laptop connected to the remote host through
a standard socket.
Figure 4.8: WiFi and 3G - WiFi being cut oﬀ
Figure 4.9: WiFi and 3G - 3G being cut oﬀ
Initially, we intended to understand how the use of multiple interfaces could aﬀect the
handoﬀ and throughput of HappyHour, that is, when ku = Both. This setup was
focused on performance and achieved the highest throughput. As such, it was intended
for situations when an application directly sent large amounts of accelerometer data
(such as in the case of our FindMe scenario). It was also useful for whenever a user
moved within a urban environment and was exposed to periodic connections to and
disconnections from WiFi hotspots; for example, whenever a user entered or left a
nightlife establishment bolstering its own wireless network.
After the connection was initiated on both interfaces, a disconnection event was
simulated by disabling one of them. This allowed us to study how the connection was
maintained by the remaining interface. Figures 4.8 and 4.9 show the throughput of the

89
TCP connection on the remote host when both interfaces were active at the same time.
As we can see in both cases, when one interface was cut oﬀ the connection eﬀortlessly
continued through the remaining interface. There was, of course, a drop on the general
throughput, but the dropped packets were quickly resent and the information continued
to ﬂow without any major problems. It is interesting to note that the maximum
achieved throughput was less than the sum of the throughput of both interfaces (we
achieved around 91% of this theoretical sum).
Figure 4.10: WiFi as primary, 3G as backup
Figure 4.11: 3G as primary, WiFi as backup
We performed a second series of tests for ku = WiFi and ku = 3 G. In these conﬁgu-
rations, one interface was conﬁgured as backup and the primary interface was disabled
mid-test. We imagined this would be a fairly popular setup amongst general users, since
WiFi usually oﬀers better performance and energy eﬃciency at a lower ﬁnancial cost.
Returning to our HappyHour use case, the user would have now deﬁned ku = WiFi
and desired to visit a diﬀerent establishment. Since the WiFi’s range is limited to the
establishment, the WiFi interface would eventually experience a connection loss. In such
an event, MPTCP would be responsible for rerouting traﬃc through 3G.

90
As shown by the graphic in Figure 4.10, the connection to the server took about 4
seconds to recover. While the connection had been already established through the 3G
interface (the initial three-way handshake and MP JOIN messages had been exchanged
[156]), the 3G subﬂow’s congestion window still had its initial value and needed some
time to adapt to the sudden increase in demand. In our tests, the WiFi loss occurred not
long after the initial connection had been established. However, in cases when a longer
time passed since 3G was used, there was the possibility for the 3G radio to enter an
idle state and, thus, require additional time to wake up. Either way, the connection was
not broken and, therefore, it was never necessary to begin a new handshaking process
under a diﬀerent IP, as it would happen if only regular TCP was being used.
Figure 4.11 shows a situation where ku = 3 G,that is, 3G has been deﬁned as the
primary interface while WiFi has been relegated to backup. Both this conﬁguration
and one where both interfaces are used together were particularly useful for vehicular
scenarios. Going back to our HappyHour use case, the user was given a ride by a friend
and seated on the passenger side, still using the application to decide where to go
next. When moving at vehicular speed (e.g. 60 km/h), connectivity for WiFi networks
becomes very transient and it would have been diﬃcult for him to obtain connections
that last more than a few seconds. This being the case, using 3G as a main interface or
using both 3G and WiFi at the same time were both good strategies for increasing the
odds of maintaining stable connections. As we can see in the tests, WiFi recovered the
connection in about 2,5 seconds, which was faster than 3G. This was expected, since
WiFi had a much greater downlink capacity and its congestion window increased much
faster.
In order to better ascertain the handoﬀ delays for each interface, we performed 10
additional measurements and computed their mean recovery time. We considered the
recovery time to be the delay between the last packet sent by the primary interface and
the ﬁrst packet sent by the backup one. The results in Figure 4.12 show that, while the
3G connection suﬀered from a larger mean recovery time (4.27 seconds), this delay was
also more stable since the values did not deviate too much from the 4 seconds mark.
On the other hand, the WiFi connection tended to recover faster (mean of 3.04 seconds)
but this recovery also ﬂuctuated more, with several experiments resulting in delays of
around 4 seconds.

91
Figure 4.12: Recovery times for each interface
4.2.5 In summary...
In conclusion, this work was an experimental proof-of-concept that served as ground
for the development of human-aware network-selection mechanisms. Considering how
our own “brute-force” evaluations resulted in disconnection periods as high as 10 sec-
onds and how previous research had presented 8 second handover delays for standard
MobileIP [154], we deemed MPTCP’s handover delays of around 3-4 seconds accept-
able. Nevertheless, we believed that better handoﬀ performance could still be achieved
through the tweaking of several settings, such as the congestion window initial values.
However, this scientiﬁc endeavor was not pursued. Instead, we focused on evolving the
architecture herein presented to a system with an even greater human-awareness. In
fact, this work represented the basis of our ﬁrst true HiTLCPS, where we enhanced the
concepts of human-aware network-selection mechanism with an emotional dimension.
This emotional-aware system, which allowed users to select the best networking inter-
faces to be automatically used depending on their mood, will be further discussed on
the next section.
Publications based on this section’s work
• D. Nunes, D. Raposo, D. Silva, P. Carmona, and J. S. Silva, “Achieving Human-
Aware Seamless Handoﬀ,” in Distributed Computing in Sensor Systems (DCOSS),
2015 International Conference on . IEEE, 2015, pp. 254–259

92
4.3 HappyWalk - Improving Mood With An Emotionally
Aware Application
Our work continued to focus on smartphone sensing within CPSs, with emphasis on
systems that sensed various facets of humans and their surrounding environments. In
particular, the detection of human emotions could lead to emotionally-aware applications
that used this information to improve people’s daily lives. For instance, a HiTLCPS
could respond to a person’s stress in order to improve their emotional state; thus, a
person’s feelings would be responsible for direct system actuation. We explored such
ideas in the work Happy Hour - Improving Mood With An Emotionally Aware Appli-
cation, where we attempted an implementation of a emotionally-aware HiTLCPS that
promoted positive moods through moderate walking exercise.
4.3.1 Emotional states and Aﬀective Computing
The emotional states of humans aﬀect their performance in learning [158], commitment
to work, their relationships with friends, family, and co-workers, as well as their mental
health and well-being. Emotional-awareness applied to these areas of e-learning or
therapy is directly related with Aﬀective Computing research. The term “aﬀective
computing” refers to systems and devices that can recognize, process and, additionally,
“have” human aﬀects.
Previous proposals in the aﬀective computing ﬁeld had used sensors for emotion recog-
nition. In this regard, facial expressions, posture, speech, body temperature, even the
strength and pace of keystrokes, had been employed in conjunction with machine learn-
ing algorithms [159]. Diﬀerent types of approaches had met diﬀerent levels of success.
For example, real-time emotion detection based on facial recognition had been shown
to be technically diﬃcult, due to the complexity of image processing algorithms and
the inherent physiological distinctions between diﬀerent humans expressing the same
feeling [160]. In speech, Paeschke [161] reported a 77% classiﬁcation accuracy in recog-
nizing agitation and calmness using neural networks, applied to a call-center scenario.
Physiological signals were used in [162] to identify negative and positive emotions with
87% accuracy, as well as in [159] to identify states of high and low arousal with 95%
accuracy. A pilot study by Samsung [163] suggested the motivation of the smartphone
manufacturer in detecting a user’s emotion through simple touching behavior. Some
research has combined several of these techniques with some success. For example, a
research work presented in [164] fused facial recognition and speech analysis, with results
showing signiﬁcant improvements in the performance and the robustness of the emotion

93
recognition system. This suggested that fusing several diﬀerent types of information
could lead to more a accurate detection of emotions.
4.3.2 HappyWalk Application
In this work, we proposed an evolution of the HappyHour app previously presented
in section 4.2.3 which, in its later iterations, came to be known as HappyWalk. We
developed this app with a therapy context in mind; more precisely, it could be described
as a Behavior Change Intervention (BCI) system that improved human physical and
mental well-being using smartphones.
BCIs are therapeutic systems that focus on providing advice, support and relevant
information in order to motivate the correction of prejudicial behaviors. Traditionally
associated with presential therapeutic consultations, BCIs had recently begun to be
delivered through the Internet and smartphones [165]. Using smartphone sensors to
monitor humans in BCIs not only helps in providing more eﬀective feedback to help
the user in correcting their prejudicial behavior, but also helps behavioral scientists’
research.
Previous research had found evidence that moderate walking exercise and the change
of environments can contribute to the improvement of mental health, providing several
cognitive beneﬁts such as improved memory, attention and mood [166] [167]. Other
studies suggested that contact with natural environments not only makes people feel
better but also makes them behave better, thus presenting both personal health beneﬁts
and broader social beneﬁts [168].
HappyWalk drew on this premise; our objective was to use a machine learning algorithm
to infer the user’s current emotion and use this information to trigger suggestive feed-
back that motivated walking exercise. The walking exercise would, in turn, positively
aﬀect mood. Collaborative data gathering was also employed to determine the near
real-time context of nearby points-of-interest (POI) that could be of interest to visit.
Additionally, our system dynamically adapted privacy settings and the smartphone’s
network interfaces based on the user’s mood. Figure 4.13 represents the HiTL mecha-
nism implemented by HappyWalk. Human emotions were periodically inferred and the
result was compared with feedback provided by the user himself. This feedback served
as input for the system’s self-learning process, which would reduce the number of user
feedback requests as future inference tasks became more accurate. Depending on the
inference result, the system assumed diﬀerent behaviors. Negative emotions prompted
the system to suggest walking exercises and, at the same time, a map was shown with
possible destinations. As previously mentioned, this emotional information also aﬀected

94
Figure 4.13: HappyWalk application ﬂow
the system’s privacy settings and the device’s network interfaces. If the user’s emo-
tion was positive, the system applied the associated rules and attempted to revert the
countermeasures applied to the negative emotional states.
From an high-level point-of-view, the HappyWalk system was very similar to Happy-
Hour (previously presented in 4.2.3). It was composed by an Android application, a
main Server and a Web Server. There were two types of persons that interacted with
the system, regular users and managers of POI (such as nightclub owners or museum
personnel), as seen in Figure 4.14. Similarly to HappyHour, POI managers used a web
Figure 4.14: HappyHour/HappyWalk high-level architecture
interface (this time, developed in Ruby On Rails) to manage descriptions, events (e.g.
“Dinossaur exposition”; “80’s night”) and other useful information regarding a partic-
ular POI. Managers could also schedule notiﬁcations (referring to cultural events or
public notices) to be delivered to all HappyWalk users in the surrounding area (within

95
700 meters) at a speciﬁc time. Additionally, managers could publish these events and
notiﬁcations to their Facebook, Twitter and Google+ Accounts, in order to reach a
wider audience.
The main server, implemented in Java, handled requests for creating, updating or delet-
ing users, events, notiﬁcations and points of interest. It was also responsible for the
connection with the Foursquare4 and Facebook5 APIs, from where it fetched POI infor-
mation. The server oﬀered its functionality in the form of RESTful web services, both
to the Android App and to the Management web interface. It also communicated with
a database where records of users, POI locations, descriptions and events were kept.
The core of our HiTLCPS was HappyWalk’s Android application. Because of the co-
existence of many versions of the Android OS, there was a concern to ensure that the
application could work with as many versions of Android as possible. We chose the
Android SDK 2.3.3 with Google API’s because it ensured a compatibility with 99.3%
of Android handsets, according to Google’s statistics 6. The Android application was
Figure 4.15: HappyWalk Android application
composed by several “Activities”, responsible for interacting with the user, and one
4https://developer.foursquare.com/
5https://developers.facebook.com/
6https://developer.android.com/about/dashboards/index.html?utm source=ausdroid.net

96
background service, responsible for syncing information with the server, showing no-
tiﬁcations, sensing the user and the environment and processing the raw sensor data.
Figure 4.15 shows how each of its components related to each other.
The User feedback activity was responsible for acquiring the user’s feedback on the
emotion detection result. The map activity displayed the map, heatmaps, points of
interest, and current position. When negative user emotions were detected, suggesting
user activities provided suggestions for the user to go for a walk. A music recogni-
tion background service processed the ambiance sound and attempted to recognize
background music through a web music recognition API. The privacy and network
manager classes were responsible for applying the privacy and network proﬁles asso-
ciated with each emotion. A neural network class received sensory input information
and performed emotion recognition, training itself based on feedback provided by the
user. Finally, there were also classes that processed data from sensors (agitation,
heart-rate, sound) and external services, namely weather information.
The application was, thus, responsible for four main tasks:
• periodically acquiring sensory information and processing this data through a neu-
ral network to infer emotions;
• dynamically adapting privacy settings and networking interfaces through an emo-
tionally aware rule system;
• interacting with the user through a map and showing relevant information regard-
ing nearby POIs;
• periodically acquiring and sending GPS position, music identiﬁcation and ac-
celerometer data to the central server.
We will discuss each of these tasks over the next subsections.
4.3.3 Monitoring human emotions
The core of our emotion-awareness lied in our ability to process diﬀerent forms of sensing.
From the beginning, our objective was not to propose robust methods for emotion de-
tection, but instead, to provide a practical proof-of-concept that showed how emotional
information could beneﬁt HiTLCPSs.

97
4.3.3.1 Sources of Input
An important matter was to decide which sources of input were to be used. Our current
understanding still does not yet provide an exhaustive picture of which factors inﬂuence
a person’s emotions [159]. Thus, we intended to consider at least three general sources
of data: environmental clues, vital-signs information and meteorological information.
Regarding environmental clues, obvious choices of sensors were those already provided
by the smartphone device. In particular, the accelerometer and microphone had been
previously identiﬁed as eﬀective sensors for identifying human context [95]. Thus, our
application acquired raw data from these sensors and processed it through a simple
classiﬁer that performed a FFT on the raw data and summed the fourier coeﬃcients.
This sum gave the neural network an idea on the amount of background noise and
movement detected by the smartphone. Unfortunately, this information was limiting, in
the sense that it only referred to conditions regarding the user’s immediate surroundings.
Other important measures lied closer to the user’s internal body functions. In fact,
heart-rate had been previously associated with emotion recognition [169]. To bring this
information to our system, we used a wearable smartshirt 7 that relayed the user’s ECG
signal through Bluetooth, which was then processed to infer the current heart-rate.
Finally, previous research had shown links between emotional states and weather con-
ditions [170]. To consider these links, the application fetched the current temperature,
precipitation and cloudiness directly from a weather API 8.
4.3.3.2 Machine-Learning Algorithm
In order to determine a good machine learning technique for our application, we studied
previous comparisons between the diﬀerent possibilities. Previous work [19], the results
of which are shown in table 4.1, had scored diﬀerent classiﬁcation algorithms in terms
of correct classiﬁcation rate and in terms of central processing unit (CPU) time needed
for the classiﬁcation. The latter is of particular importance for smartphone HiTLCPSs,
since these are limited terms of available processing power and energy. This led us
to opt for an artiﬁcial neural network as our emotion inference tool, since it oﬀered
a reasonable correct classiﬁcation rate while being one of the least time-consuming
techniques. Other good alternatives could be C4.5 decision trees and Support vector
machines, but ANNs make it signiﬁcantly easier to update our inference model with
7VitalJacket: http://www.vitaljacket.com/
8Open Weather Map: http://openweathermap.org/

98
Machine learning
techniques
Correct classiﬁcation
rate
Ranking of less CPU
time needed for
classiﬁcation
Random Forest 96.5% 5th
Support vector machines 80.2% 1st
Naive Bayes classiﬁers 81.5% 6th
Bayesian networks 90.9% 4th
Logistic regression 83.4% 3rd
Artiﬁcial neural networks 87.2% 2nd
Instance-based classiﬁers
(KStar, LWL, and IBk). 95.6%, 95.5% and 80.32% 8th, 9th, 7th
Table 4.1: Machine learning approach for sensing contexts in Smartphones[19].
new data provided by the user.
The neural network was implemented using the Encog 9 machine learning framework
[171]. When designing its architecture, we found that selecting a number of hidden
neurons that provided minimal error and highest accuracy was a challenging task. Pre-
vious research had shown that excessive hidden neurons will cause over ﬁtting; that is,
the neural networks may overestimate the complexity of the target problem. A rule of
thumb is to select a number of hidden neurons between the size of input neurons and
the number of output neurons [172]. Thus, we decided to test two possibilities: a hid-
den layer with 4 neurons or two hidden layers, with three neurons in the ﬁrst and two
neurons in the second. Both of these conﬁgurations ﬁt onto the rule of thumb without
becoming overly complex and taxing for smartphone hardware. In our decision process,
we considered two major requirements: the amount of eﬀort required for training the
network (which was important in terms of processing power and battery drain) and the
accuracy of the network.
In order to test the training eﬀort, we began by generating simulated emotions. For
each type of emotion, we empirically deﬁned a probability value for diﬀerent ranges
of its input components (heart rate, cloudiness, movement, etc). This method allowed
us to generate 150 simulated emotions, that, while not valid for testing accuracy, were
suﬃcient for testing training performance. Thus, we counted the number of epochs
necessary to successfully train the network for each conﬁguration. The results, show in
table 4.2, suggested that using two hidden layers increased the training eﬀort signiﬁ-
cantly. Therefore, we also needed to test if using more layers brought any beneﬁts in
terms of accuracy. To do so, we requested a test subject to use our application for a pe-
riod of a week, during which his sensory data and emotional feedback were recorded for
9Encog Framework: http://www.heatonresearch.com/encogin

99
Conﬁguration Number of epochs
One hidden layer 100
Two hidden layers 3000
Table 4.2: Testing training performance (150 emotions).
a total of 41 records. We then tested both neural network conﬁgurations using this data,
to evaluate their sensitivity and speciﬁcity. These experiments indicated that using a
Conﬁguration Sensitivity Speciﬁcity
One hidden layer 0,679 0,766

Table 4.3: Testing neural network accuracy (41 emotions).
two layer conﬁguration presented considerably better performance, as shown in table
4.3. After pondering over the results, we decided that, despite being more demanding,
a two layer conﬁguration presented a better compromise in terms of training time and
accuracy. Thus, we opted for a conﬁguration with two hidden layers, the ﬁrst contain-
ing three nodes and the second with two nodes. The implemented neural network’s
architecture is presented in Figure 4.16.
Figure 4.16: HappyWalk’s emotional neural network.
4.3.4 User Feedback Strategy
A feedback request consisted of three steps: data collection, state-inference, user feed-
back and network training. Data collection consisted on getting the data described in
section 4.3.3.1, while inferring the user’s mood was performed using the ANN archi-
tecture described in section 4.3.3.2. After that, a notiﬁcation appears asking the user
for feedback, shown in Figure 4.17. After the user provides feedback a training phase

100
is initiated, where the feedback valued is used to train the network through resilient
propagation.
Figure 4.17: HappyWalk’s Emotional Feedback
In our implementation, we considered four distinct moods: “euphoria”, “calmness”,
“boredom” and “anxiety”. “Boredom” and “Anxiety” were considered “negative” emo-
tions, whereas “Euphoria” and “Calmness” were considered their “positive” counter-
parts. Users received a notiﬁcation when an emotion was detected and, by selecting
it, the application opened and displayed the feedback screen, as shown in Figure 4.17.
The output representing the inferred emotion was shown as a yellow circle in a two-
dimensional circular space containing the four emotions. The user could provide cor-
rective feedback by dragging the yellow circle to a new position, now shown in green.
This feedback initiated a re-training process, in which the ANN adjusted its weights
to improve its accuracy. From the ﬁgure, we can see that our emotional feedback was
essentially a 2 axes plane: the X axis (width) corresponds to an Anxiety-Calmness value,
whereas the Y axis (height) corresponds to an Euphoria-Boredom value.
It is known, from previous research, that an increase in feedback requests will, most
likely, lead to greater user frustration [173]. If we repeatedly request user feedback,
there is no point to our learning mechanism: the main focus of our HiTL app should
be to become as unobtrusive as possible. As such, we wanted to minimize feedback
requests by only performing them when necessary. We can achieve such strategy using
a very simple algorithm:

101
