where:
• output and idealOutput - are arrays containing the ANN outputs and the user
feedback, respectively;
• βprevious - is the previous feedback period (previous value of β);
• eucdistcurrent - is the euclidean distance between the last emotion inference and
the corresponding user’s feedback;
• eucdistmean - is a weighted mean calculated from previous eucdistcurrent values;
• wnewEucDist - is a weight representing the impact of the newest eucdistcurrent on
eucdistmean;
• eucdistmax - is the maximum euclidean distance;
• βnew - is a temporary value computed from a direct linear variation based on
eucdistmean;
• βmax and βmin- are the maximum and minimum feedback periods, respectively;
• β - is the period until the next feedback request;

102
• wnewEmoTime - is a weight representing the impact of βnew on β;
After some experimentation we empirically set the values of wnewEucDist and
wnewEmoTime to 0.4, βmax and βmin to 1 and 48 hours (respectively), and the initial
values of βprevious and eucdistmean to βmax+βmin
2 and eucdistmax
2 (respectively). An em-
pirical approach was used because we believed that an awareness of the impact of the
ﬁnal β value would be important before attempting to ﬁne-tune our feedback strategy.
Such a study, however, was outside of our scope and left for future work.
4.3.5 Emotionally aware rule system
Depending on the emotion classiﬁcation result and the user’s feedback, the HiTL control
assumed diﬀerent decisions. These measures were the responsibility of the “emotionally
aware rule system”, which was implemented based on sets of actions. Users speciﬁed
proﬁles for each emotional state, and actions associated with each proﬁle. These proﬁles
allowed the application to automate and specify privacy and networking conﬁgurations
in accordance to a HiTL paradigm. In particular, if a negative emotional state was
detected (e.g. high anxiety or boredom), the default behavior took direct measures to
improve the user’s mood.
Privacy conﬁgurations allowed users to create rules that governed the automatic sharing
of location in social networks. We developed this feature from the assumption that
diﬀerent people have diﬀerent ways of dealing with negative emotions. Some people
may feel that negative emotional states are eased by social-interaction. In these cases,
the automatic sharing of location among the user’s friends could contribute towards
positive socialization. Other people prefer solitude to ease their minds and thus, might
want to refrain from publishing their location in a social network. This way, the privacy
proﬁles implemented a HiTL approach for managing privacy settings.
Network interface conﬁgurations were also governed by a emotional proﬁles. When the
user’s emotions were positive, the standard network proﬁle deﬁned that WiFi was to
be used as the primary interface while cellular communications were used as a backup.
This was because cellular communications usually involve additional monetary costs.
However, if the system entered a negative emotion state, the standard network proﬁle
deﬁned that cellular connections and WiFi would be used at the same time. For example,
the user could set the conﬁguration to use two interfaces (3G/4G and Wiﬁ) whenever
he felt more anxious. We developed the system this way for two main reasons.

103
Firstly, to reduce possible sources of frustration that users might have experienced, re-
sultant from intermittent connectivity issues [174]. These issues could occur whenever
a user lost connection to a WiFi hotspot, or entered an area that did not have cellular
coverage. In these situations, the established TCP connections would be interrupted and
the device would need to reestablish a new session on the remaining interface, which
could take a considerable time. However, as we concluded from our previous study, dis-
cussed in section 4.2.4, using both interfaces simultaneously on the same transport-layer
session means that TCP sessions can continue eﬀortlessly through the remaining network
interface, providing a better Quality of Experience (QoE). Users that were emotionally
distressed might have been willing to pay the additional price of cellular connectivity,
in order to reduce frustration and thus, contribute towards the improvement of their
mood.
The second reason was of a more technical nature and was due to the additional data
being sent to the server. When the human was on a negative mood, his/her position and
emotional states were monitored more frequently, in order to provide better feedback
and improve the impact of the positive reinforcement resultant from walking suggestions.
Using several network interfaces provided better reliability in data collection during
these negative states. Nevertheless, the network proﬁle that governed these changes
was completely conﬁgurable by the user in case he wished to completely disable cellular
connections, for example.
Maintaining a single TCP session while using several networking interfaces and perform-
ing seamless network handoﬀ between them was achieved through MPTCP [151]. This
was one of the limitations of our implemented prototype: deﬁning rules that used more
than one interface at the same time required the smartphone to have an MPTCP-enabled
kernel. Additionally, while the rules did aﬀect applications other than HappyWalk, this
would only happen if those applications were MPTCP-compatible.
4.3.6 Near-Real time POI information
The map interface from HappyWalk was re-adapted to this new use-case (see Figure
4.18).
However, instead of being limited to nightlife clubs and bars, it also displayed other
possible walking destinations, including parks or museums. Each place of interest was
represented though an icon, which was aggregated into a cluster of icons when the map’s
zoom was low. These clusters were represented as circles displaying the number of ag-
gregated places. Just like in HappyHour, the application rendered real-time information
about each place, namely the attendance of other users (overlayed as a number on top

104
Figure 4.18: HappyWalk’s map screen.
of the POI icon) and its average mood (using heatmaps of colors associated with certain
emotions). Users could still browse a list of events associated with the POI, which was
maintained by the place’s managers through the management web-interface. Managers
could also schedule notiﬁcations that were to be delivered to all HappyWalk’s user’s in
the surroundings. The primary purpose of these notiﬁcations was to advertise current
cultural activities, initiatives, events, or promotions that might have been of interest to
the public.
The app also allowed for the detection of music currently being played (music detection
was particularly interesting for bars and nightclubs). Information about music being
played at each point of interest was acquired using the smartphone’s microphone. Sound
samples were processed, generating a ﬁngerprint which was then identiﬁed through a
web-based music recognition API 10. This feature could have been of interest to users
who wished to visit bars or establishments where certain types of music were played
that better vented their emotional stress.
All of this near real-time POI information was made possible by automatic periodic
sharing (every 5 minutes), of the user’s location, emotion and acoustic ﬁngerprinting.
Since some of this information may be sensitive, the sharing was made in an non-
identiﬁable fashion. The information provided allowed users to identify the best place
in a local area to visit. Since every person has their own music tastes and emotional
10Echoprint: http://echoprint.me/

105
responses, providing detailed information about the environment of each POI could have
had an impact in the user’s decision. For instance, a person could choose a crowded bar
with music if they wanted to dance, or decide to visit a more quiet nature park for a
relaxing walk, instead.
4.3.7 In summary...
HappyWalk drew from our past experience in near-real time POI characterization and
people-centric sensing. At the same time, it also explored concepts from areas that were
still unfamiliar to us, such as aﬀective computing. Not that these concepts were com-
pletely new; in fact, previous research had already approached many of HappyWalk’s
ideas. EmotionSense [165], for example, was a mood tracking framework used for experi-
mental sociology and psychology, which collected data about how users felt. The system
used sensors (movement, proximity of other devices and location) and voice recognition
for the detection of emotions and speaker identities. Users were asked to ﬁll a form twice
a day, where they marked their mood using a “emotion grid”. The researchers found
that the distribution of the emotions detected through EmotionSense generally reﬂected
the self-reports by the participants. On the other hand, VibN [175] also explored the
idea real-time POI information by exploiting multiple sensor feeds. The application al-
lowed its users to explore cities by presenting real-time information on hotspots, derived
from sensor data. Each hotspot was also characterized by a demographics breakdown
of inhabitants and a list of short audio clips.
HappyWalk’s emotion inference model was inspired in the emotion grid system from
EmotionSense and the near real-time information about places draws many parallels
with what was proposed in VibN. However, in our eyes, it innovated by being the
ﬁrst work that combined these aspects with a proactive approach that not only moti-
vated the user to take walks and to discover diﬀerent places, but that also adapted the
smartphone’s behavior. This ability of promoting the improvement of mood through
moderate physical exercise, emotional-awareness and near real-time information about
places, made it, as far as we know, unlike any other collaborative App of the time. Ad-
ditionally, HappyHour’s HiTL paradigm, where emotion was used to adapt application
and smartphone conﬁgurations, was a novel aspect. In other words, HappyWalk had a
strong HiTL actuation component.
In retrospective, while the inference of emotions is still a developing area, in today’s
world where users wear smartwatches, smartshirts, smartphones and other smart
wearables, the process of gathering the user’s contextual data is becoming greatly
simpliﬁed. With the increase of available data there will be a proliferation of promising

106
applications, progressively more aware of the user’s emotional state. These can have
diverse goals, such as improving the user’s mood, promote socialization or even improve
learning and working tasks. As such, we hope that this work can serve as a beacon to
guide further research into this exciting new area of HiTL-awareness.
We continued to hone our ideas while focusing on a more humanitarian aspect. The
next section will describe how HappyWalk further evolved into a system that combined
HiTL concepts with social innovation.
Publications based on this section’s work
• P. Carmona, D. Nunes, D. Raposo, D. Silva, J. S. Silva, and C. Herrera, “Happy
hour-improving mood with an emotionally aware application,” in Innovations for
Community Services (I4CS), 2015 15th International Conference on. IEEE, 2015,
pp. 1–7

107
4.4 WeDoCare: A Humanitarian People-centric Cyber-
Physical System for the beneﬁt of Refugees
The European migrant crisis began in 2015, when a rising number of refugees and
migrants made the journey to Europe to seek asylum and a better lifestyle. According
to the UN Refugee Agency [176], the number of forcibly displaced people worldwide
reached 59.5 million at the end of 2014, the highest level since World War II with a 40%
increase taking place since 2011. By 2015, more than 644,000 refugees and migrants had
arrived in Europe by sea. Of these, just over a third – 34 per cent – were women and
children who are particularly vulnerable to abuse as they transit Europe, especially in
overcrowded reception sites, or in many locations where refugees and migrants gather,
such as parks, train stations, bus stations and roadsides. Refugees, migrant women
and children traveling on their own were also at heightened risk as they move through
Europe, sometimes at night, along insecure routes or staying in places that lack basic
security.
Several applications had been previously created to tackle such issues. For example,
Safelet was a bracelet that allowed users to warn others that they were in danger [177].
The bracelet contained a button that, once triggered, emitted an alarm to the user’s
friends and family and prompted the user’s smartphone to begin recording audio. An-
other similar solution was Athena [178], a round button that clipped onto a persons
belt and that emitted loud alarms whenever pressed. The device also could also send
text messages containing the user’s location to previously designated emergency con-
tacts. While both solutions are similar, Athena can be considered more inconspicuous
since it can be more easily hidden from potential persecutor. However, both of these
solutions require extra hardware the user has to acquire, hence only being available to
the economically advantaged. This means that such solutions may not be ideal for the
refugee demographic, displaced people who may be going through economic diﬃculties.
Additionally, emergency contacts may be unable to help, due to not being in the user’s
surroundings.
In the face of such a scenario, we wondered if we could apply the same HiTL principles
we had developed to create a humanitarian system that could somehow help protect
these women and children. As such, we had a meeting with elements from the United
Nations High Commissioner for Refugees 11 programme where we were informed that
most refugees carried a smartphone with them. Since we wanted to use technology-based
solutions within most people’s reach, we once again decided to develop a smartphone-
based solution.
11http://www.unhcr.org/

108
Any smartphone can potentially be turned into a personal security device, allowing users
to detect emergency situations around them 24 hours a day. However, there is a catch:
because monitoring should be carried out continuously, the monitoring process will easily
drain the smartphone’s battery if the detection algorithm and monitoring intervals aren’t
carefully designed to reduce power consumption. Therefore, it is important to achieve a
balance between the smartphone’s battery life and the application’s background work.
Thus, in this work we intended to study the impact of sensor readings intervals with the
following contributions:
• We presented our own HiTL smartphone application: WeDoCare, a HiTLCPS
that used mobile sensors to detect emergency situations in real-time.
• We studied how the frequency of location, sensory readings, and signal processing
might aﬀect the smartphone’s battery life in our scenario.
4.4.1 WeDoCare App
WeDoCare was a HiTL application that used the smartphone’s sensors, in particular
the microphone, to detect, in an automated way and in real-time, situations of violent
attacks against users through a scream detector. A system of this kind had a diﬀer-
ent approach to Athena and Safelet in the sense that it did not require the user to
press buttons; screams allowed for a more natural and faster reaction to danger. This
scream recognition task ran on a background service which collected the data from the
microphone and extracted certain features (data acquisition), sent these features to a
classiﬁcation system which decided if the microphone’s output was a scream or not
(state inference), and ﬁnally sent an alarm depending on the situation (actuation). The
smartphone’s GPS was also used to retrieve the location of the individual, which was
attached to the alarm information.
Alarms were sent to surrounding citizens that also used the app, giving them a chance
to help and collaborate by helping others stay safe – IoT being used for humanitarian
purposes. Location information was used to decide to which users notiﬁcations were
sent. In our prototype notiﬁcations were sent to the users within a 2km radius of the
alarm’s location.
Concerning the user’s interface, the application showed on the map areas where attacks
were more frequent. These “danger zones” were represented by red markers whose trans-
parency rate depended on the danger level associated with that location. Higher trans-
parency levels are associated with zones where attacks occur more frequently (higher
risk) and vice versa. The user could also choose to use heatmaps for danger intensity

109
Figure 4.19: WeDoCare screens
representation, if he so desired. Helpful locales such as hospitals and police stations
were also shown, using blue markers. These were initially populated by using Google’s
Geolocation API [179].
Any user could add new danger zones or helpful locales, with additional information
such as type of locale or danger level. These crowd-sourced additions were restricted to
three per a day, per user. This decision was made to prevent possible spam of malicious
additions to the map. If a user was ﬂagged as a system abuser, he/she was automatically
added to a blacklist and prevented from adding new map pins for a certain amount of
time.
Some of the application’s interface screens are shown in Figure 4.19.
When a user approached a danger zone the application emitted a notiﬁcation warning
that he/she could be in danger. It then suggested the user to take an alternative route.
With this feature users, we attempted to promote safety by motivating users to avoid
areas where attacks tended to be more common. This closed the human-in-the-loop, as
the app actuates to prevent further danger, preserving the security of the individual.
When developing WeDoCare, we did not want to restrict its applicability solely to refugee
victims. Such a system could also be applied in the context of other social scenarios such

110
as attacks on women in South America, elderly healthcare, among others. In fact, at the
time of this document’s writing we continue to strongly believe in WeDoCare’s potential
scalability and are currently looking towards applying it to other diﬀerent humanitarian
scenarios.
Our prototype was developed for Android smartphones since it was the one most
used worldwide. This is important since we wanted to reach the maximum number of
citizens as possible. We used Apache Tomcat 7 as a server management system and as
a bridge between the mobile phone and the database. For database management we
used Hibernate and PostgreSQL.
4.4.2 Scream recognition
The dominant characteristic in emergency situations or attacks is screaming (non-
linguistic vocalization). Previous research had been done where mobile phones’ mi-
crophones had been used detect screams or gunshots [180][181][182]. In this work, we
did not contribute towards the improvement of these previous solutions but, instead,
we aimed towards using state-of-the-art scream recognition techniques within our larger
HiTLCPS. To validate our algorithms we acquired screaming sounds from 40 volunteers,
recording from smartphones placed in their pockets.
The ﬁrst step in our automatic scream recognition system was to extract features, i.e.
identify the components of the audio signal that are of interest and ﬁlter out everything
else, such as talking, crying, glass breaking or background noise. After the features were
extracted, they were sent to a classiﬁcation system which decided if the audio signal
was, or not, a scream. No ﬁlters were applied before feature extracting; this is because,
nowadays background noise reduction is found in nearly every microphone built-in to
the smartphone, which helps to conduct conversations clearly.
Most of the sound energy from human speech is contained in the 55Hz-5kHz range [183].
According to the Nyquist–Shannon theorem, the sampling frequency of a signal that can
subsequently be reconstituted with minimal information loss should be greater than or
equal to twice the bandwidth of the signal. Since our application processes audio in
real-time we restricted our sampling rate at 11025Hz, which is still greater than twice
of the highest frequency typically considered for human speech range.
The scream recognition system we implemented used an architecture that joined oper-
ations in a ﬂexible and simple processing pipeline, shown in Figure 4.20.

111
Figure 4.20: WeDoCare’s sound processing pipeline.
To contribute towards application performance we aimed towards keeping the audio
processing pipeline simple and only used a single audio channel continuously streaming
from the microphone, for as long as WeDoCare was running. To process the signal,
the dispatcher chopped the incoming audio into individual blocks. Choosing the best
block size in this phase is an important step for audio processing. Larger block sizes
are more diﬃcult to process and may add larger delays while smaller ones might not
provide enough information for classiﬁcation purposes. In our case, we chose a block
size of 1024 samples with an overlap of 512 samples (50% of the total block size).
This size provides a practical trade-oﬀ between computational performance, update
speed and usability and is a common approach [184]. This means that each audio
sample was a block of 1024 /11kHz ≈ 92.88ms. From our dataset, we empirically
veriﬁed that screams typically more than 0.5 seconds long. As such, an event was
only considered a scream if at least 6 consecutive sample blocks were classiﬁed as such12.
The dispatcher then sent the block of audio through a series of audio processing algo-
rithms. In the processing section, sound analysis was made and features of the audio
signal were extracted. As mentioned above, audio was acquired at a sample rate of
11kHz, from which we extracted several features.
The ﬁrst feature extracted was the amount of energy in the signal block, which can be
computed as logarithmic dB measurement. This is equivalent to computing the sound’s
pressure level (dBSPL), where the eﬀective pressure of the sound is calculated relative
to a reference value. In our case, we followed an implementation similar to the one
proposed by the TarsosDSP library [185]; the energy of a block of audio b, of length n,
was computed as:
20 log10
√∑n
i=0b[i]
n
Such a feature allows for the distinction between high-energy sounds (such as screams)
from many low-energy sounds, such as normal speech [180]. However, when comparing
sounds with similar levels of energy it becomes less eﬀective. Additionally, the amount
of sound energy detected by a microphone is unreliable by itself. This is because the
energy contained in a sound drops with the increase in distance between the source of
the sound and the smartphone’s microphone [181]. Therefore, we felt the need to use
12since 500ms/92.88ms≈ 5.4

112
other types of features.
One of these other features extracted from the processor block was the sound pitch
(Hz). For pitch estimation we used the YIN method [186]. As we have veriﬁed by
analyzing our own data, sound pitch is typically higher in screams than in regular
speech. However, not all screams are the same, varying signiﬁcantly in terms of
frequency. In our data, screams usually fell into the 300-2200Hz range. Women screams
tended to be more acute, in the 1000Hz-2200Hz range, while mans’ fell more into the
300Hz-800Hz range. These observations were in accordance to the traditionally voice
frequency band ranges used in telephony (approximately 300 Hz to 3400 Hz) [183].
As such, we decided to perform a pre-ﬁltering of sounds by their pitch. Sounds were
only sent into the classiﬁcation system when their pitch exceeded 300Hz. This avoided
unnecessary computation and helped saving battery.
The detection of high pitch and high energy values removed most of the background
noise and other low frequency sounds, but these weren’t enough to detect screams ac-
curately. There are many sounds that have similar pitch values or energy values. Take,
for example, a baby’s crying or an ambulance emergency sound; these sounds have very
similar energy values but are completely diﬀerent from screams (noise-wise). As such,
using only these two features could result in many false alarms being reported. There-
fore, to increase the accuracy of the application, we introduced MFCCs (Mel-frequency
cepstral coeﬃcients) – we used a standardized MFCC algorithm deﬁned in the early
2000s by the European Telecommunications Standards Institute [187].
The MFCCs are commonly used in speech recognition applications to characterize blocks
of sound. In fact, the introduction of MFCCs to our system increased scream recognition
accuracy signiﬁcantly. The default parameters for MFCC calculation were used; 13
cepstrum coeﬃcients were obtained using 26 mel ﬁlters in the ﬁlter bank. We also
applied a lower ﬁlter (0Hz) and an upper ﬁlter (with half the frequency of the sample
rate) for the band edge of the mel ﬁlters. These parameters work fairly well for most
cases. Delta features were not used.
4.4.3 Experiments and Evaluations
We performed two types of experiments of the WeDoCare system. On a ﬁrst phase,
we tested diﬀerent types of machine learning techniques to discover which was better

113
adapted towards our scream recognition use-case. On a second phase, we performed sev-
eral tests to evaluate the impact of WeDoCare’s location awareness on the smartphone’s
battery usage.
4.4.3.1 Scream Recognition
To validate WeDoCare’s scream recognition, we constructed a dataset by asking 40 vol-
unteers to scream. Scream sounds were recorded with the smartphone in the volunteer’s
pocket, since normally this is where people carry it. We also collected various sounds
similar to screams, such as baby cries, ambulance sounds or music, to train against false
positives. This dataset was then used to train and test diﬀerent types of classiﬁers. It
was divided into two subsets, a training subset containing 4500 samples, and a testing
subset with 504 samples.
In an initial phase, we trained and tested our classiﬁcation system with a dataset
including regular day-to-day audio events, such as speech, buses and cars passing by,
background noise, etc. These samples have very diﬀerent characteristics from a scream
event and their pitch typically does not exceed 300Hz. As previously explained, our
classiﬁcation system included a pitch threshold ﬁlter that set 300Hz as the minimum
value to be considered. This meant that most day-to-day audio events were correctly
classiﬁed as non-screams. We then trained and tested the classiﬁcation system with
sound events very similar to screams (pitch wise), such as a baby crying, sirens, whistles,
glasses toasting, loud TV sounds, ringtones and dogs howling. The performance of our
classiﬁcation system was measured by calculating both its accuracy and F1 score.
In this work we decided to experiment with ANN and SVM as classiﬁcation algorithms
for scream recognition, both being popular strategies for supervised machine learning.
This choice derived from SVM being previously used for scream recognition in the
literature [180] and our own past experience with ANN (see section 4.3). Through our
experiments we tested diﬀerent conﬁgurations of these algorithms on our dataset to
discover which approach was best suited for our use-case.
We used the Encog machine learning framework [171] to implement our SVM classiﬁers.
The eﬀectiveness a SVM depends on the selection of the kernel and its parameters, as well
as of the penalty factor C. We experimented with the linear, sigmoid, polynomial and
radial basis function (RBF) kernels. As for the kernel parameters we only considered
gamma, γ, which has diﬀerent eﬀects on the behavior of the RBF, polynomial and
sigmoid kernels. All other kernel parameters were left at their default values.

114
To choose the best penalty factor, C, and gamma, γ, for each kernel, we conducted a
“grid-search” using cross-validation and exponentially growing sequences of C and γ,
as suggested in [188]. For the linear kernel the best value of C was 0.5. For both the
polynomial and the RBF kernels, the best γ values were the same: 1.0E− 4. As for the
penalty factor, RBF had its best C at 32 and the polynomial at 8. For the sigmoid, no
optimalC andγ values were found, all of them gave similar results. In fact, the sigmoid
kernel did poorly on classifying our problem and its performance was much worse than
the other alternatives. As such, we decided to discard it. Table 4.4 shows the accuracy
and the error rate of scream event recognition for the remaining kernel types. The linear
kernel performs acceptably but is surpassed by both polynomial and RBF, with the RBF
having a slight advantage.
Kernel Type Linear Polynomial RBF
Sensitivity /
Recall 87.56% 99.08% 99.54%
Speciﬁcity 90.24% 97.91% 97.91%
Precision 87.16% 97.29% 97.30%
Accuracy 89.08% 98.41% 98.61%
F1 Score 87.36% 98.17% 98.41%
Table 4.4: Scream recognition results using various SVM kernel types.
We then compared our best-performing SVM against an ANN approach, also imple-
mented using the Encog framework [171]. As previously mentioned in section 4.3.3,
selecting a good ANN architecture is challenging due to the limitations of mobile hard-
ware and issues such as over ﬁtting. Drawing from our past experience with HappyWalk,
we decided to use two hidden layers with a sigmoid activation function. We then tested
various ANN conﬁgurations with diﬀerent numbers of hidden neurons each. Once again,
we used cross-validation (with the same testing data used for the SVM evaluation) to
determine an acceptable number of training iterations for each architecture. We tested
in increments of 500 iterations (with a maximum of 5000) to select an adequate training
eﬀort that did not incur in over ﬁtting. Table 4.5 shows the best results obtained for
each of the tested conﬁgurations.
Interestingly, both 16-100-100-1 and 16-8-8-1 conﬁgurations achieved very good results,
with 16-100-100-1 requiring less eﬀort to train. Additional training iterations for 16-
100-100-1 led to worse results, likely due to over ﬁtting.
To have an idea of the computation complexity involved for each approach, we times-
tamped training and testing processes for each approach. These tests were performed
on a laptop with an Intel 5Y51 1.1Ghz CPU and 8GB of RAM. Figure 4.21 shows the
results for the top performing solutions.

This situation illustrates the diﬃculty in selecting good ANN conﬁgurations; deeper net-
works tend to provide better results, but dealing with thinner networks may sometimes
oﬀer the best compromise in terms of performance/accuracy for some use-cases. In our
case, we are interested in having a scream detector as accurate as possible. Therefore, we
chose the SVM-RBF classiﬁer, since it yielded the best overall classiﬁcation performance
while maintaining an acceptable computation complexity.
4.4.3.2 Location Battery Consumption
For a safety monitoring application, power drain is an important metric. Particularly
in a refugee scenario, the power necessary to recharge a smartphone may not be readily
available. As such, it was important to mind the amount of energy being consumed by
WeDoCare, so that the application did not become an inconvenience for its users. From
our experience, we identiﬁed two major factors that probably had the greatest impact in
terms of power consumption in our application: the frequency of data acquisition/s-
tate inference and the type of localization accuracy being used. However, since
WeDoCare was a safety monitoring app, it was not acceptable to compromise on scream

116
recognition uptime. Therefore, decided to keep it a continuous process and focused on
optimizing localization, instead.
Smartphone location in WeDoCare was achieved through the Google Play Services Loca-
tion API. Using this API it is possible to set the priority of the location request and
the rate (interval in milliseconds) at which this request is made. We ran several tests on
battery consumption under diﬀerent conﬁgurations of these two factors. Throughout all
tests we used a mid-range smartphone, the Samsung Galaxy S4 Mini. Internally, the S4
Mini features a dual-core 1.7 GHz Snapdragon 400 processor with 1.5 GB of RAM, 8 GB
of expandable storage, and a 4.27 inch qHD (540 x 960 pixel) Super AMOLED screen
with Android KitKat 4.4.2. Its approximate idle battery life is 220 hours (smartphone
on standby). This value was used as a reference throughout the result analysis. To
measure the smartphone’s battery we used Android’s BatteryManager 13 class to pool
the current battery level every hour.
It is also important to note that we stopped all non-critical background tasks and apps
to ensure that only WeDoCare, Google Play Services, and the required OS tasks were
running in the smartphone. The operation environment of the tests was also taken into
account: all the tests were executed in the same place and using the same WI-FI access
point to avoid possible inconsistencies due to changes in Wi-Fi and GPS connectivity.
Google Play Services deﬁned three possible variants for location priority:
• PRIORITY LOW POWER – This setting has an accuracy of approximately
10 kilometers. We considered this level of accuracy to be too coarse and, thus,
discarded this setting.
• PRIORITY BALANCED POWER ACCURACY – This setting has an ac-
curacy of approximately 50 meters and is likely to consume less power. With this
setting, the location services use Wi-Fi and cell tower positioning.
• PRIORITY HIGH ACCURACY – This setting uses GPS to achieve precise
location, with an accuracy of typically less than 10 meters.
We began by comparing the PRIORITY BALANCED POWER ACCURACY setting
with the PRIORITY HIGH ACCURACY one keeping the location update interval at
1 minute. As shown in Figure 4.22, using PRIORITY HIGH ACCURACY results in
a reduction of battery life by 93,2%, which is a diﬀerence of 8.7% when compared to
PRIORITY BALANCED POWER ACCURACY. Since WeDoCare app should be as
13https://developer.android.com/training/monitoring-device-state/battery-monitoring.html

117
accurate as possible, we decided that a 8.7% diﬀerence in battery usage an accept-
able compromise for more precise location. We then focused on determining the most

adequate rate at which location updates were to be requested. We kept the priority

In the prototype developed for this work, we decided to adopt a variable temporal
acuity and let the user make the ﬁnal decision. In the application’s settings we enabled
an option that allowed users to choose between these three location collection rates,
warning that greater accuracy implies greater battery drainage.

118
4.4.4 In summary...
In this work we proposed and evaluated an innovative HiTLCPS that attempted to
improve a concrete social issue. We attempted to tackle refugee security by promoting
crowd-sourced actuation in case of danger. Our scream-recognition system was designed
and validated through various tests that compared diﬀerent machine learning techniques.
More speciﬁcally, we studied various types of SVMs and ANN conﬁgurations. We ob-
tained good results with both techniques, with SVM having a small advantage. Our
initial results appear to indicate that, for a scream-recognition use-case, thin ANN con-
ﬁgurations may achieve the best compromise between performance and computation
complexity; however, further research would have to be performed in order to validate
this claim.
Additionally, we studied the impact of diﬀerent location collection strategies in terms
of battery usage. We found that it is possible to trade spacial acuity for temporal
acuity while maintaining similar battery usage levels. In the developed prototype, we
left the level of location accuracy as a choice for the end user. Nevertheless, it could be
interesting to research a dynamic location accuracy that changes according to the user’s
context. For example, the application could increase the rate of location collection
as the user approaches a dangerous zone, or if an attack was detected in the user’s
surroundings. The study of dynamic location collection techniques, as well as real-world
testing of WeDoCare were left for future work.
Publications based on this section’s work

V. Pereira, H. Dias, C. Herrera et al., “WeDoCare: A humanitarian people-centric
cyber-physical system for the beneﬁt of refugees,” in Global Humanitarian Tech-
nology Conference (GHTC), 2016 . IEEE, 2016, pp. 213–219

119
4.5 On Resource Optimized Deep Reinforcement Learning
for Embedded and Mobile Devices with Applications
to Control and Networking
We have previously identiﬁed State Inference and Actuation as some of the most
challenging aspects of HiTL control. Most of the experimental works presented in
this chapter, including HappyWalk and WeDoCare, attempted to apply machine
learning techniques for state inference, with a particular preference towards artiﬁcial
neural networks. However, both HappyWalk and WeDoCare dealt with rather simple
ANN architectures, to avoid burdening the mobile device with heavy computational
tasks. Nevertheless, we felt that it was worth considering the possibility of applying
more powerful ANN architectures in HiTL scenarios. The usage of large-scale ANNs
is a practice often associated with the concept of Deep Learning [189]. Similarly
to shallow ANNs, deep learning ANNs use layers of nonlinear processing units for
feature extraction and transformation, with each successive layer using the output
from the previous layer as input. Deep ANNs, however, use many of these layers, each
containing a great number of processing units. This approach typically leads to a
better abstraction of the multiple levels of features present in the data, which in turn
enables more complex modeling of non-linear relationships and better classiﬁcation
results. In fact, in the past ﬁve years deep learning has made important strides within
several learning tasks, including speech recognition [190], object classiﬁcation [191], and
language translation [192].
Within deep learning we believe that Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL), in particu-
lar, is a technique that has the potential of being successfully applied towards solving
both State Inference and Actuation tasks in HiTLCPS. Fundamentally, and just like
preceding decades of earlier forms of reinforcement learning, DRL oﬀers the ability to
learn algorithms that make decisions on the basis of sensory inputs. In a reinforcement
learning problem, as illustrated in Figure 4.24, an agent tries to maximize the reward
that it is receiving from making decisions. The reinforcement algorithm has only two
sources of feedback to learn from: penalty (pain of failing) and reward (thrill of achiev-
ing the objective increasingly better). The value of each state is represented by a value
function that provides estimates that the agent can use to make proper decisions at each
step. For example, in a racing scenario negative rewards would be given for all states
where the car’s position is oﬀ-track and positive rewards are given for completing a lap.
However, let us consider a scenario with an autonomous robot is trying to reach a
certain destination in a factory where humans also operate. The number of possible

120
Figure 4.24: Simpliﬁed view of Deep Reinforcement Learning.
states quickly becomes nearly inﬁnite and it is unfeasible to create a value function for
them all; it would take too long to compute all partial rewards for every possible speed
and position on all the factory’s paths while also considering all the relative positions
and actions of the other robots and human workers. DRL are reinforcement learning
solutions that use Deep Learning techniques for the agent’s decisions (e.g., the agent’s
implementation is a Deep Neural Network). In the autonomous robot example, the
“state” could be represented by the deep neural network, which just takes all the state
variables (position, front camera image, etc.) and outputs actions for each moment in
time.
DRL has recently achieved many celebrated results around ability to train algorithms
that reach human-level performance across a wide-range of games of skill. For example,
Deep Q Learning (DQN) accomplished such a feat with≈ 50 Atari games [193] without
any need to customize the learning algorithm or underlying neural architectures from
game to game. DRL has also been successfully use in games that have long resisted
automated cognition such Go, with AlphaGo [194], and poker, with DeepStack [195].
The possible applications of DRL go well beyond the ability to play games. Since it is
a technique intimately related with the notion of learning how to perform control tasks,
a DRL implementation could be used to learn both state inference (value function) and
actuation (actions) of autonomous agents within HiTLCPSs. However, DRL has not yet
been applied to a variety of these possible tasks performed by resource constrained sen-
sor nodes, smartphones, robots and drones; the reason for this is because of the extreme
system resources DRL implementations typically require. Most of the existing examples
of CPS applying these techniques have access to huge resource budgets. For instance,
the control decisions of autonomous cars have already adopted DRL techniques [196];

121
however, platforms such as the Nvidia Drive PX2 [197] designed for such scenarios re-
quire 250W of power while oﬀering 8 tera-ﬂops of computation. As an extreme example,
for AlphaGo to beat the world’s best human player using these methods required more
than 1,200 CPUs and 176 GPUs [194].
So far, we have been dealing with HiTLCPSs that have the smartphone has the main
bridge between the human user and the CPS. The ability to feasible apply DRL-style
algorithms in embedded and mobile processors would be broadly beneﬁcial to a range
of existing tasks required by HiTLCPS sensor networks, smartphones, robots and
drones. However, we lacked a principled understanding of how to reduce and manage
the resources used by DRL in order to target these scenarios. It was still unclear
how well these algorithms would perform when potential avenues of optimization were
explored; could acceptable control and decision performance be maintained within the
constraints of mobile and embedded devices?
While searching for answers to these questions, we had the opportunity to work together
with Professor Nicholas D. Lane and research student Rui Luo from University College
London, as well as with research scientist Sourav Bhattacharya from Nokia Bell Labs
Cambridge. We collaborated during my three-month visit to University College London
and achieved several results related to DRL optimization. In this work, we performed to
the best of our knowledge the ﬁrst exploration towards general-purpose system resource
optimizations techniques for DRL algorithms. Our aim was to enable this class of
algorithms to execute even on severely constrained embedded processors such as the
ARM (Advanced RISC Machine) Cortex M0 [198] by the reducing memory, computation
and energy requirements. Under our approach such eﬃciency gains largely came at the
expense of a manageable loss in algorithm performance (i.e., the ability to ﬁrst learn,
and then perform control and decision tasks).
While many optimization approaches have been proposed for deep learning models de-
signed for classiﬁcation problems (e.g., [199] [200]), DRL presents a mixture of unique
resource bottlenecks, along with diﬀering algorithmic data-ﬂows and neural network ar-
chitectures that prevented the direct application of these methods. Unlike deep neural
network classiﬁers that are typically trained oﬀ-line before they are introduced to the
constrained device, DRL often revises model parameters iteratively based on the arrival
of new data; as a result, not only must the forward-pass of the neural networks used in
DRL be optimized, but also those used for back-propagation.
In this section, I will detail my own contributions towards this scientiﬁc work:

122
• Key Early Insights into DRL for Embedded-class Platforms. We showed
through the use of the general-purpose optimization techniques reasonable trade-
oﬀs of algorithm performance (i.e., reward function) and system resources (e.g.,
memory). This opens the door to many new powerful applications of DRL for
embedded devices within HiTLCPS and oﬀers a foundation for further research
into DRL optimization.
• Systematic Study of Resource-optimized DRL with Application agnos-
tic Techniques. Through the study of the most popular form of DRL today –
DQN ; I applied and evaluated the use of two optimization techniques: redundancy
minimization through the singular value decomposition method, and recurrent
neural network reduction. When combined, these methods lowered the resource
needs of DRL to the point where it could locally run on embedded hardware;
signiﬁcantly, none of these techniques are tied to speciﬁc DRL tasks.
• End-to-end Evaluation within Emerging Applications of Embedded
DRL. I worked on evaluating embedded DRL within two distinct end-to-end
applications. Firstly, I experimented with several OpenAI Gym scenarios [201],
of which CartPole will be detailed. Secondly, I tested a networking co-ordination
task in which nodes use DRL to learn a custom message-passing protocol for a
topology and environment [202]. We showed this recent theoretical result can be
executed (through use of optimization techniques) even on a network of ARM
Cortex M0 nodes.
4.5.1 Related Work
Optimization in reinforcement learning is not a novel idea and several researchers ap-
proached this problem since the early 2000s. In fact, in [203] the problem of large state
and action spaces had already been considered as a limitation. The authors proposed
a mechanism for solving the states generalization problem in reinforcement learning al-
gorithms through a clustering mechanism based on the vector quantization technique
for signal analog-to-digital conversion and compression, and on the Generalized Lloyd
Algorithm for the design of vector quantizers. Additionally, they also presented the
VQQL model, that integrated Q-Learning as reinforcement learning technique and vec-
tor quantization as state generalization technique. Another optimization example can be
found in [204]. Computational operations in reinforcement learning algorithms increase
exponentially with the cardinality of the state space, hence their very long training
times. To ease this issue, the authors also applied vector quantization to adaptively and
incrementally partition the state space based on the recent estimate of the action-value
function.

123
More recently, other types of approaches to reinforcement learning optimization have
surfaced. In [205] a new indirect encoding scheme for neural networks was proposed,
where the weight matrices were represented in the frequency domain by sets of Fourier
coeﬃcients. The authors exploited spatial regularities in the matrix by ignoring high-
frequency coeﬃcients, similarly to lossy image compression, thus reducing its dimen-
sionality. The eﬃciency of the approach was tested using the CoSyNE neuroevolution
algorithm on three benchmark problems: pole-balancing, ball throwing and octopus-arm
control.
In [206] the authors dealt with the problem of high-dimensional input spaces in
neuroevolution approaches. Instead of resorting to the traditional approaches such as
compressing the neural network controller representation or employing pre-processors
that transform raw input into low-dimensional features, the authors transformed the
high-dimensional visual input into a compact feature vector through a deep, max-
pooling convolutional neural network. This way, they were able to evolve extremely
small recurrent neural network controllers for a task that previously required networks
with over a million weights. Their work was validated in “The Open Racing Car
Simulator” [207] where they successfully to controlled a car using only visual input.
While these approaches are certainly interesting for developing future optimizations
for embedded forms of DRL, none were developed to target the reduction in resources
needed for severely constrained HiTLCPS devices and can not be directly applied to
DQN networks and its subsequent derivatives. More closely related to our exploration
are the latest innovations in optimizing DRL policies. Such optimization was tackled in
[208], where the authors proposed a new method called policy distillation that can be
used to extract the policy of a reinforcement learning agent and train a new network
that performs at the expert level while being dramatically smaller and more eﬃcient.
According to the paper, policy distillation allows for the network size to be compressed
by up to 15 times without degradation in performance. The technique also allows for
multiple expert policies to can be combined into a single multi-task policy that can
outperform the original experts. So far this method has been explored only within Atari
games and so is unclear how it may apply to the tasks of interest to embedded systems.
Nevertheless, it is a concrete example of DRL-focused optimizations that are distinct
from discriminative deep neural network optimizations.

124
4.5.2 DQN Resource Optimization Overview
The main objective of this work was to understand existing bottlenecks in the state-of-
the-art DQN algorithms and develop eﬃcient schemes that allowed running DQN on
resource constrained IoT and embedded devices. The development of these schemes was
the area of specialty of Professor Nicholas D. Lane and research scientist Sourav Bhat-
tacharya. While I was not directly involved in their conception, I applied these schemes
to achieve the results presented in the next sections. In fact, such ANN optimization
knowledge is outside of the scope of my PhD thesis and, therefore, this section will only
provide a general overview on the nature of the optimization schemes that I worked with.
Deep neural networks contain high degree of redundancy due to over parametrization. It
is often possible to reduce these redundancies present in fully connected layers to achieve
a more compact model. Compacted models require a lesser number of computations to
execute, which results in decreased power requirements. Typically, compression can be
applied on the connections between any two successive (fully connected) linear layers.
All connections between layer L (containing m units) and L + 1 (containing n units)
are represented by a matrix WL, where the ith row of WL contains all the connection
strengths from ith unit in layer L to all units in L + 1.
Redundancy reduction can be achieved through a matrix factorization technique that
operates on these connection parameters WL between two successive layers. The idea
is to factorize WL into two components, U and V , such that WL≈ U· V . The overall
memory requirement to store the factorized components is smaller than the original
weight matrix itself and the factorized approach needs fewer operations.
In this work we employed the well knownSingular Value Decomposition(SVD) technique
to factorize the the weight matrix WL+1
m×n of two adjacent layers (L and L + 1) with m
and n units respectively:
WL+1
m×n =Um×m Σm×nNn×n (4.1)
WL+1
m×n was approximated by keeping k biggest singular values:
ˆWL+1
m×n = Um×k Σk×kNk×n (4.2)
ˆWL+1
m×n = Um×kVk×n (4.3)
Therefore,WL+1
m×n can be replaced by the product of Um×k andVk×n , which is achieved
by introducing a new layer with k≪m,n units between layer L and L + 1. Because L

125
andL+ 1 units are fully connected, the introduction of the new layer allows the number
of pairwise calculations and weight parameters to decrease, when:
k< m·n
m +n (4.4)
A second type of redundancy reduction applied in this work was the decrease in size of
recurrent neural network (RNN) layers. A RNN is a type of artiﬁcial neural network
where connections between neurons have loops that allow information to be carried be-
tween them. Each neuron has a form of internal memory and is capable of maintaining
information about previous input. This creates networks that are capable of maintain-
ing an internal state, which allows them to exhibit dynamic temporal behavior. Such
networks are typically applied to tasks such as handwriting or speech recognition. In
our case, they were applied to a networking co-ordination task in which two agents ex-
changed information about numbers recognized by each [202]. By compressing the size
of the RNN layers we were able to achieve agents that performed at the level of full-sized
ones while requiring considerably less resources to execute.
4.5.3 Evaluation Experiments
Interpreting the performance of DRL algorithms is something inherently related with
the nature of the scenario being learned. Nevertheless, the reward score within known
scenarios is the typical metric used by the reinforcement learning community to compare
diﬀerent implementations. Diﬀerences between reward scores can be interpreted as
diﬀerences in the quality of agent decisions. That is to say, a drop of 10% in the reward
score suggests that the decisions made are 10% worse. We use such an interpretation
when analyzing our results; we perform a comparison between the reward scores achieved
by the optimized DRL agents, and the original baseline agent.
In section 4.5.2 we described the optimization approaches being used. As a means
of referencing the lowering of complexity through the use of optimization techniques
we adopt the term retain rate. In our particular case, this refers to the amount of
ANN parameters retained in the overall architecture relative to the original model. A
retain rate of 0.80, for example, means that through the applications of methods such
as matrix factorization technique, only 80% of the original number of parameters remain.

126
4.5.3.1 OpenAIGym’s CartPole
We began by evaluating our agents within an environment open and consistent across
the reinforcement learning community. We used OpenAI Gym [201] to implement a
reinforcement learning scenario that is a common benchmark for evaluating new agent
algorithms, CartPole. This allowed us to easily compare our scores against hundreds of
previously submitted ones from the community and have a better understanding of the
quality of the reward values being achieved. By varying the retain level (and resulting
lowering of resource consumption) of the optimizations we were able to observe the
trade-oﬀ in decision quality in a repeatable and systematic environment.
The objective of CartPole is to keep balance of a pendulum and prevent it from falling
over by controlling a cart to which the pendulum is attached by an un-actuated joint (see
Figure 4.25). The cart is placed on a frictionless track on which it moves to the left or
the right side. The pole starts upright, and tends to fall over. The objective of the agent
is to control the cart by sending signals of moving left or right to prevent the pole from
falling. The episode fails whenever the pole deviates more than 15 degrees from vertical
or the cart moves more than 2.4 units from the center. A reward of 1 is obtained for
every frame the pole is kept upright. The total score is the cumulative points it earned
during each episode. As the episode has the maximum length of 200 frames, the upper
bound of the reward is 200. The observation of this game is a tuple of [cart-displacement,
cart-velocity, pole-angle, pole-angular-velocity ] while the control signal is either +1 or
−1 indicating an instantaneous force pointing right or left, respectively.
Figure 4.25: An illustration of CartPole.
The model we used for CartPole was rather small as the input and output were quite sim-
ple. It consisted of 4 stacked fully-connected layers: the ﬁrst 3 (hidden) fully-connected
layers, each with rectiﬁer nonlinearity, contained 16 units while the last one (the linear
output layer) contained 2 units corresponding to the binary action. Since we were only
dealing with fully-connected layers in a standard ANN, we only used SVD factorization,
and applied it to the ﬁrst 3 (hidden) fully-connected layers.
We trained the model during 175k episodes and then applied the SVD optimization to
the fully-connected layers. We tested the original model and the factorized model with

127
diﬀerent retain levels, namely 0.95, 0.80, 0.65, 0.50. The result is depicted in Figure
4.26 where the cyan curves illustrate the episodic scores whereas the red ones show the
30-episode average scores in 1000 testing episodes.

Figure 4.26: Performance of diﬀerent cartpole models.
As Figure 4.26 shows, we found that the performance of the 0.95 retain rate is com-
parable with that of the original model. In fact, the performance of 0.95 retain rate is
slightly better than the original one as it clearly avoids some of the sudden collapses in
the reward time series, which makes the average score higher. We believe this is caused
by the redundancy minimization removing some noise within the model. The perfor-
mance starts to decrease when the retain level is lower than 0.95 and reaches about 15
reward at a retain rate of 0.50.
One may also notice that the performance of 0.65 retain rate is better than that of
0.80. One interpretation of this result rests on the nonlinearity of ANNs. It is possible
that smaller variations within the model (those removed within the interval [0.80, 0.95])
are responsible for subtle but crucial actions whereas greater variations (corresponding
to interval [0.65, 0.85]) may actually be redundant. In other words, we believe that the
importance of certain variations within a DRL agent’s architecture cannot always be cor-
related with their size. This means that DRL optimization through matrix factorization
is not trivial and possible nonlinearity eﬀects may have to be taken into consideration.

128
4.5.3.2 Distributed Coordination Network Protocol
The aim of this set of experiments was to optimize parts of the DQN solution presented
in [202]. In this work, the authors attempted to demonstrate that it was possible for
diﬀerent agents to learn communication protocols in order to share information needed
to solve speciﬁc tasks. We focused on their Diﬀerentiable Inter-Agent Learning (DIAL)
approach applied to the Colour-Digit MNIST game.
The Colour-Digit MNIST is a game played by two agents in which each agent observes
the pixels from a random MNIST (Modiﬁed National Institute of Standards and Tech-
nology) database digit [209] in red or green. The color and digit values are hidden, so
each agent has to recognize the number and its color from the raw pixel information. It
is a two-step game where in the ﬁrst step, both agents send a 1-bit message, and in the
second step they select a binary action. The reward for each agent consists of two com-
ponents that are antisymmetric in the action, color, and parity (odd, even) of the digits.
Since only one bit of information can be sent, agents must agree to encode/decode either
color or parity, with parity yielding greater rewards.
Since it was diﬃcult to estimate the actual upper bound of protocol behavior, the
objective of our optimizations was to reach as closely the performance of the original
agent behavior deﬁned by [202] as possible. This was our baseline agent, achieved
without constraint on resources. The baseline agent was trained for 5000 episodes,
when it reached an acceptable performance level.
In our ﬁrst experiment, we applied the two optimization techniques (SVD optimization
and RNN reduction) to the baseline agent and tested the resulting agents for 5000
iterations. The baseline agent uses 128 RNN nodes per hidden layer. Applying RNN
reduction simply results in an agent whose hidden layers have been compressed to a
lower number of RNN nodes. However, as explained in section 4.5.2, matrix factorization
through SVD results in additional layers being created. As an example, table 4.6 shows
the original baseline input model (used to identify the MNIST numbers, contains two
large linear layers) with the one resulting from SVD compression.
Model Architecture
Baseline v:1*{1568}; bn:1 †; h:1 ‡{1568}; rl:1 §; bn:1; h:1{128}; v:1{128}
SVD Optimized v:1{1568}; bn:1; h:2{1568, 103}; rl:1; bn:1; h:2{128, 82}; v:1{128}

Table 4.6: Comparison of original and optimized MNIST recognition models.

129
Figure 4.27 shows a comparison of the average reward score for the baseline agent
and two optimized variations, a compression of the RNN layers of the agent (called
RNN 32 ) and redundancy compression through SVD (called Weight compression 65 ).
The optimization techniques were equally applied to both Colour-Digit MNIST players.
This experimental result shows that the overall reward score remains similar, despite
the compression.

Figure 4.27: RNN 32 and Weight compression 65 compared to the Base model.
Figure 4.28 shows the training of the baseline agent (no changes to its architecture)
when compared to two compressed agents as described above. It is interesting to note
that the training behavior is very diﬀerent between RNN 32 and Weight compression
65. In the case of RNN 32 there is a clear impact on the eﬀort required by the agents to
learn the necessary protocol behavior to achieve good results. While the baseline agent
can learn how to communicate properly in ≈ 1200 episodes, RNN 32 takes almost≈
4500 episodes to achieve a comparable result. This is likely due to the fact that RNN
size reduction also implies a reduction on the memory capacity of the agents; each agent
is less able to remember information from past episodes. Additionally, the reduction in
network size also means that the agent has to relearn everything from scratch. Since the
very nature of its architecture is diﬀerent, all the weights have to be relearned. Still, it is
important to remember that this is a one-time only cost that is only experienced during
agent training. There is also another important factor to be taken into consideration:
as shown in 4.29, the system performance of RNN reduced algorithms is greater and
each training epoch is faster to compute. Therefore, the actual elapsed time diﬀerence
between RNN 32 and the baseline is not as signiﬁcant as suggested by this purely epoch
view.

130
On the other hand, Weight compression 65 adapts the original baseline agent’s archi-
tecture and simply reduces the network’s redundancy through SVD factorization. It
appears as if the small changes made to the weight representation structure have al-
lowed this agent to converge more quickly. In other words, this agent retains an amount
of memory closer to the baseline agent’s and is probably capable of partially reusing the
baseline agent’s previous training eﬀort. In fact, Weight compression 65 is capable of
re-adapting from its redundancy reduction is just ≈ 600 episodes.

4.5.4 In summary...
In this work, we studied a number of optimization directions for reducing the system
resource usage of DRL towards embedded-scale HiTLCPS deployments. It should be
viewed as an early attempt towards what we believe to be an important future area
of study, and there are likely many more potential venues to be considered. The ap-
proaches examined here were resultant from Professor Nicholas D. Lane and research
scientist Sourav Bhattacharya’s experience with inference-stage optimization of deep
neural network classiﬁers. However, as discussed above, optimization for DRL and deep
classiﬁers may diﬀerent in many respects. As such, we expect for more DRL-speciﬁc
optimization opportunities to be discovered and evaluated in future research.
Additionally, this study was focused exclusively on the DQN algorithm, currently the
most popular form of DRL in the literature. There are, however, many other algorithmic
designs, including dueling network architectures [210], double Q-learning [211] and actor-
critic approaches [212]. Nevertheless, in many aspects these works are extensions of the
original DQN concept. As such, and while this remains to be proven with further
experiments, we anticipate that the insights demonstrated in this work will still apply
in these more advanced approaches. Still, expanding beyond DQNs will, most likely,
provide new opportunities for richer optimization options.

133
4.6 In summary...
In this chapter, we described the experimental work and associated scientiﬁc publications
behind this PhD thesis. While initial works were still very much associated with the
concept of “people-centric sensing”, later works began to tackle HiTLCPSs more fully.
There is also a clear progressive investment in the usage of smartphones and machine-
learning, which is due to their progressive evolution during the PhD.
This list of developed work does not, by any means, provide a complete incursion into
the area of HiTLCPSs, as its reach is far too wide. Instead, we provide several results
and conclusions that clearly show how smartphones can, and will most likely continue to
be, the most important bridge between the IoT framework, Web technologies and social-
networking, and the human element. We also show how machine-learning techniques
such as ANNs and SVMs can be feasibly run within these constrained devices and how
their usage is an invaluable tool to understand the human condition.
In the next chapter we will stand upon the experience gained from our theoretical
research and practical experience to draw an analysis on the remaining challenges and
on the impact of future HiTLCPS research.

Chapter 5
Conclusions and the Future of
Human-In-the-Loop
Cyber-Physical Systems
After looking at their trajectory, theory, and practice, in this chapter we will conclude
our journey through human-in-the-loop cyber-physical systems by discussing their likely
evolution, given the state-of-the-art. We begin by presenting a summary of the scientiﬁc
contributions provided by our work in section 5.1. In section 5.2 we will delve into the
requirements and challenges faced by emerging and future HiTL applications. Both
developers and users should be aware of them, as they are crucial for any human-centric
application. Afterwards, in section 5.3, we will look at the constraints that aﬀect HiTL
systems and review the lessons learned throughout the thesis, in order to understand how
the remaining challenges may shape the next steps of HiTL research and development.
5.1 Summary of Scientiﬁc Contributions
As we brieﬂy listed in section 1.4, this PhD thesis provided various novel scientiﬁc
contributions.
Chapter 2 provided a general survey on the evolution and current state of human-in-
the-loop applications and technologies. This is an extension of our previously-published
survey paper [46], and, as far as we know, the ﬁrst attempt at such a generalized overview
on HiTL research. This survey also included an analysis on HiTLCPS requirements,
challenges and constraints which we expand upon in sections 5.2 and 5.3.
135

136
We also proposed a novel taxonomy for human roles within HiTLCPSs in Chapter 3.
This taxonomy was initially proposed in our survey paper [46], and it also is, as far as
we know, the ﬁrst of its kind. Instead of reﬂecting the type of HiTL control in place, as
attempted by previous taxonomic eﬀorts, our approach focused on understanding the
human roles within HiTLCPSs and how to best explore them.
In chapter 4 we described our experimental work, which served as basis for several
scientiﬁc publications. We started by exploring the concept of “people-centric sensing”
and later evolved into the creation of several HiTLCPS smartphone applications which
used complex machine learning algorithms. On this end, we also worked on optimizing
DRL to facilitate the use of HiTL concepts in embedded devices.
Our experimental work was the subject of a few awards and mentions. HappyWalk
[48] was presented in the I4CS’15 conference during July 2015 in Nuremberg, Germany,
and this presentation earned the conference’s “Young Scientist Award”. WeDoCare [49]
was submitted to the European Social Innovation Competition 2016, ﬁnanced by the
European Commission and placed as one of 30 semi-ﬁnalists chosen from 1095 entries
from across Europe. As a result, we were invited to present the application to the
competition’s jury during July 2016 in Berlin, Germany.
Finally, from the experience gained during the PhD we also authored an international
educational book [47], being published by the prestigious academic publisher John
Wiley & Sons. It is meant to be a primer on the topic of HiTLCPSs for college-level
students, researchers, professors, and professional developers. This thesis shares
many of its objectives and theoretical research with this book. However, they are
fundamentally diﬀerent when it comes to a more practical perspective. While this
thesis focuses on illustrating HiTL concepts as they have been applied on our own
experimental work, the book intends to introduce its readers to the practical usage of
HiTL paradigms within software development. To do so, it presents a tutorial part
where an Android application is endowed with HiTL control. The objective of this
tutorial is to begin with a bare-bones map application to a system capable of rough
estimations about the user’s current mood, providing suggestions to improve their
physical and mental well-being. This tutorial is heavily based on our own personal
experience with HappyWalk, described in section 4.3, and is available as a (conﬁdential)
annex to this thesis.
Below follows a list of the most relevant scientiﬁc publications achieved during the
